People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s more just that sitting there starts to feel uncomfortable or hard to keep doing, and I feel pulled to get up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Sometimes it’s just to refill my water or walk over by the printer, but I feel like I can’t just sit there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to get up during meetings is causing you any problems or distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in performance reviews, and I think it affects how clients see me too. One client complained, and that really worried me because I don’t want to damage accounts or make people think I don’t care. I do care a lot about doing good work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you have to, or because you just really want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. I mean, I can choose what excuse it is—getting water, stepping over by the printer—but in the moment sitting through a long meeting just feels really hard, like I need to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or worries that are repeating in your mind when you feel the need to get up during meetings?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think it’s a specific worry repeating in my head. It’s more just that sitting there starts to feel uncomfortable or hard to keep doing, and I feel pulled to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings helps you avoid something unpleasant or makes you feel better in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does make me feel better in the moment. Like, during long sales strategy meetings, I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—refilling my water or just pacing near the printer. It’s not like I’m trying to avoid the meeting exactly, it’s more that sitting there starts to feel really hard, and getting up gives me some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and do something in a very specific way, like always refilling your water or always pacing near the printer?\n\nPatient: No, not in a specific way. It’s not like I have to refill my water every time or pace in the exact same spot. Those are just easy excuses, I guess. The main thing is that during long sales strategy meetings, I feel like I need to get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up during meetings to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s to prevent something bad from happening. It’s more just that in those long strategy meetings, I feel like I can’t stay seated, even though I know I’m supposed to. So I’ll get up to refill water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you're trying to make some kind of unpleasant feeling stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s basically it. It’s not some specific fear or anything, but sitting through those long sales strategy meetings starts to feel really uncomfortable, like I just need to move. So I get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated—sometimes I’ll refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because something is telling you that you must, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Not like a voice or a rule in my head saying I must. It’s more that I’m sitting there in those long sales strategy meetings, and I just feel like I can’t stay in the chair anymore. I know I’m supposed to, but I end up getting up anyway, even if it’s just to refill my water or walk by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you do on impulse, without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not really planned out. I’ll be in a long sales strategy meeting, and at some point I just get up before I’ve fully thought it through—like to refill my water or pace near the printer—even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s not like I’m trying to avoid the meeting exactly, it’s more that sitting there starts to feel really hard, and getting up gives me some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and do something in a very specific way, like always refilling your water or always pacing near the printer?\n\nPatient: No, not in a specific way. It’s not like I have to refill my water every time or pace in the exact same spot. Those are just easy excuses, I guess. The main thing is that during long sales strategy meetings, I feel like I need to get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up during meetings to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s to prevent something bad from happening. It’s more just that in those long strategy meetings, I feel like I can’t stay seated, even though I know I’m supposed to. So I’ll get up to refill water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you're trying to make some kind of unpleasant feeling stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s basically it. It’s not some specific fear or anything, but sitting through those long sales strategy meetings starts to feel really uncomfortable, like I just need to move. So I get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated—sometimes I’ll refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because something is telling you that you must, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Not like a voice or a rule in my head saying I must. It’s more that I’m sitting there in those long sales strategy meetings, and I just feel like I can’t stay in the chair anymore. I know I’m supposed to, but I end up getting up anyway, even if it’s just to refill my water or walk by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you do on impulse, without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not really planned out. I’ll be in a long sales strategy meeting, and at some point I just get up before I’ve fully thought it through—like to refill my water or pace near the printer—even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to get up during meetings because of a repeated thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like one repeated thought I can’t ignore. It’s more that in those long sales strategy meetings, I just keep feeling like I need to get up, even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Sometimes I’ll just go refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to get up during these meetings?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have a choice in theory, but in the moment it’s really hard not to. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just pace near the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting up during meetings as a habitual behavior that you've done for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does feel like a pattern at this point. The thing that really pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Even if it’s just to refill my water or pace near the printer, I still find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting through those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel scared or panicky in those meetings. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Sometimes it’s just to refill my water or walk over by the printer, but I feel like I can’t just sit there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to get up during meetings is causing you any problems or distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in performance reviews, and I think it affects how clients see me too. One client complained, and that really worried me because I don’t want to damage accounts or make people think I don’t care. I do care a lot about doing good work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you have to, or because you just really want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. I mean, I can choose what excuse it is—getting water, stepping over by the printer—but in the moment sitting through a long meeting just feels really hard, like I need to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or worries that are repeating in your mind when you feel the need to get up during meetings?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think it’s a specific worry repeating in my head. It’s more just that sitting there starts to feel uncomfortable or hard to keep doing, and I feel pulled to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings helps you avoid something unpleasant or makes you feel better in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does make me feel better in the moment. Like, during long sales strategy meetings, I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—refilling my water or just pacing near the printer. It’s not like I’m trying to avoid the meeting exactly, it’s more that sitting there starts to feel really hard, and getting up gives me some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and do something in a very specific way, like always refilling your water or always pacing near the printer?\n\nPatient: No, not in a specific way. It’s not like I have to refill my water every time or pace in the exact same spot. Those are just easy excuses, I guess. The main thing is that during long sales strategy meetings, I feel like I need to get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up during meetings to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s to prevent something bad from happening. It’s more just that in those long strategy meetings, I feel like I can’t stay seated, even though I know I’m supposed to. So I’ll get up to refill water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you're trying to make some kind of unpleasant feeling stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s basically it. It’s not some specific fear or anything, but sitting through those long sales strategy meetings starts to feel really uncomfortable, like I just need to move. So I get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated—sometimes I’ll refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because something is telling you that you must, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Not like a voice or a rule in my head saying I must. It’s more that I’m sitting there in those long sales strategy meetings, and I just feel like I can’t stay in the chair anymore. I know I’m supposed to, but I end up getting up anyway, even if it’s just to refill my water or walk by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you do on impulse, without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not really planned out. I’ll be in a long sales strategy meeting, and at some point I just get up before I’ve fully thought it through—like to refill my water or pace near the printer—even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to get up during meetings because of a repeated thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like one repeated thought I can’t ignore. It’s more that in those long sales strategy meetings, I just keep feeling like I need to get up, even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Sometimes I’ll just go refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to get up during these meetings?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have a choice in theory, but in the moment it’s really hard not to. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just pace near the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting up during meetings as a habitual behavior that you've done for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does feel like a pattern at this point. The thing that really pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Even if it’s just to refill my water or pace near the printer, I still find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting through those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel scared or panicky in those meetings. It’s more like I get restless or uncomfortable sitting there for that long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you cannot control?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I literally can’t control my legs or anything, but it feels really hard to stop myself. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I keep getting up anyway—like to get water or just pace by the printer for a minute.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just pace near the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting up during meetings as a habitual behavior that you've done for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does feel like a pattern at this point. I don’t usually feel scared or panicky in those meetings. It’s more like I get restless or uncomfortable sitting there for that long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you cannot control?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I literally can’t control my legs or anything, but it feels really hard to stop myself. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I keep getting up anyway—like to get water or just pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around during meetings to make yourself feel just right?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” no. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in performance reviews, and I think it affects how clients see me too. I do care a lot about doing good work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you have to, or because you just really want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. I mean, I can choose what excuse it is—getting water, stepping over by the printer—but in the moment sitting through a long meeting just feels really hard, like I need to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or worries that are repeating in your mind when you feel the need to get up during meetings?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think it’s a specific worry repeating in my head. It’s more just that sitting there starts to feel uncomfortable or hard to keep doing, and I feel pulled to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings helps you avoid something unpleasant or makes you feel better in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does make me feel better in the moment. Like, during long sales strategy meetings, I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—refilling my water or just pacing near the printer. It’s not like I’m trying to avoid the meeting exactly, it’s more that sitting there starts to feel really hard, and getting up gives me some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and do something in a very specific way, like always refilling your water or always pacing near the printer?\n\nPatient: No, not in a specific way. It’s not like I have to refill my water every time or pace in the exact same spot. Those are just easy excuses, I guess. The main thing is that during long sales strategy meetings, I feel like I need to get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up during meetings to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s to prevent something bad from happening. It’s more just that in those long strategy meetings, I feel like I can’t stay seated, even though I know I’m supposed to. It’s not some specific fear or anything, but sitting through those long sales strategy meetings starts to feel really uncomfortable, like I just need to move. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in performance reviews, and I think it affects how clients see me too. One client complained, and that really worried me because I don’t want to damage accounts or make people think I don’t care. I do care a lot about doing good work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you have to, or because you just really want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. I mean, I can choose what excuse it is—getting water, stepping over by the printer—but in the moment sitting through a long meeting just feels really hard, like I need to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or worries that are repeating in your mind when you feel the need to get up during meetings?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think it’s a specific worry repeating in my head. It’s more just that sitting there starts to feel uncomfortable or hard to keep doing, and I feel pulled to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings helps you avoid something unpleasant or makes you feel better in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does make me feel better in the moment. Like, during long sales strategy meetings, I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—refilling my water or just pacing near the printer. It’s not like I’m trying to avoid the meeting exactly, it’s more that sitting there starts to feel really hard, and getting up gives me some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and do something in a very specific way, like always refilling your water or always pacing near the printer?\n\nPatient: No, not in a specific way. It’s not like I have to refill my water every time or pace in the exact same spot. Those are just easy excuses, I guess. The main thing is that during long sales strategy meetings, I feel like I need to get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up during meetings to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s to prevent something bad from happening. It’s more just that in those long strategy meetings, I feel like I can’t stay seated, even though I know I’m supposed to. So I’ll get up to refill water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you're trying to make some kind of unpleasant feeling stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s basically it. It’s not some specific fear or anything, but sitting through those long sales strategy meetings starts to feel really uncomfortable, like I just need to move. So I get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated—sometimes I’ll refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because something is telling you that you must, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Not like a voice or a rule in my head saying I must. It’s more that I’m sitting there in those long sales strategy meetings, and I just feel like I can’t stay in the chair anymore. I know I’m supposed to, but I end up getting up anyway, even if it’s just to refill my water or walk by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you do on impulse, without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not really planned out. I’ll be in a long sales strategy meeting, and at some point I just get up before I’ve fully thought it through—like to refill my water or pace near the printer—even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to get up during meetings because of a repeated thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like one repeated thought I can’t ignore. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just pace near the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting up during meetings as a habitual behavior that you've done for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does feel like a pattern at this point. The thing that really pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Even if it’s just to refill my water or pace near the printer, I still find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting through those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel scared or panicky in those meetings. It’s more like I get restless or uncomfortable sitting there for that long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you cannot control?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I literally can’t control my legs or anything, but it feels really hard to stop myself. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I keep getting up anyway—like to get water or just pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around during meetings to make yourself feel just right?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” no. One client complained, and that really worried me because I don’t want to damage accounts or make people think I don’t care. I do care a lot about doing good work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you have to, or because you just really want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. I mean, I can choose what excuse it is—getting water, stepping over by the printer—but in the moment sitting through a long meeting just feels really hard, like I need to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or worries that are repeating in your mind when you feel the need to get up during meetings?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think it’s a specific worry repeating in my head. It’s more just that sitting there starts to feel uncomfortable or hard to keep doing, and I feel pulled to get up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings helps you avoid something unpleasant or makes you feel better in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does make me feel better in the moment. Like, during long sales strategy meetings, I know I’m supposed to stay seated, but I end up getting up anyway—refilling my water or just pacing near the printer. It’s not like I’m trying to avoid the meeting exactly, it’s more that sitting there starts to feel really hard, and getting up gives me some relief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and do something in a very specific way, like always refilling your water or always pacing near the printer?\n\nPatient: No, not in a specific way. It’s not like I have to refill my water every time or pace in the exact same spot. Those are just easy excuses, I guess. The main thing is that during long sales strategy meetings, I feel like I need to get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up during meetings to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s to prevent something bad from happening. It’s more just that in those long strategy meetings, I feel like I can’t stay seated, even though I know I’m supposed to. So I’ll get up to refill water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because you're trying to make some kind of unpleasant feeling stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s basically it. It’s not some specific fear or anything, but sitting through those long sales strategy meetings starts to feel really uncomfortable, like I just need to move. So I get up even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated—sometimes I’ll refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up during meetings because something is telling you that you must, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Not like a voice or a rule in my head saying I must. It’s more that I’m sitting there in those long sales strategy meetings, and I just feel like I can’t stay in the chair anymore. I know I’m supposed to, but I end up getting up anyway, even if it’s just to refill my water or walk by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you do on impulse, without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s not really planned out. I’ll be in a long sales strategy meeting, and at some point I just get up before I’ve fully thought it through—like to refill my water or pace near the printer—even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to get up during meetings because of a repeated thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like one repeated thought I can’t ignore. It’s more that in those long sales strategy meetings, I just keep feeling like I need to get up, even though I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Sometimes I’ll just go refill my water or pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to get up during these meetings?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have a choice in theory, but in the moment it’s really hard not to. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I end up getting up anyway—like to refill my water or just pace near the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting up during meetings as a habitual behavior that you've done for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does feel like a pattern at this point. The thing that really pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during long sales strategy meetings when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. Even if it’s just to refill my water or pace near the printer, I still find myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting through those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel scared or panicky in those meetings. It’s more like I get restless or uncomfortable sitting there for that long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up during meetings is something you cannot control?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I literally can’t control my legs or anything, but it feels really hard to stop myself. I know I’m supposed to stay seated in those long sales strategy meetings, but I keep getting up anyway—like to get water or just pace by the printer for a minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around during meetings to make yourself feel just right?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” no. I don’t think it’s about needing things to feel a certain way.
